Nottingham Forest didn’t need to splash the cash to replace Anderson: Oliver Glasner has done it again

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When Nottingham Forest sanctioned Elliot Anderson’s £116m transfer to Manchester City over the summer, many expected the Tricky Trees to delve into the market to replace the England star. After all, Anderson was Forest’s key man last season. In a season of inconsistency in the dugout, one consistency for the Midlands side was the 23-year-old’s performance levels.

Forest were backed to take a chunk of the fee they received for Anderson and splurge on a new central midfielder. Lucas Bergvall was linked with a move to the City Ground. So, too, was Tijjani Reijnders. The former remained at Tottenham Hotspur, and has since signed a new deal at the Tottenham Hotspur Stadium. The latter moved to Saudi Pro League side Al-Qadsiah.

Oliver Glasner’s side weren’t necessarily forced to look from within in order to replace Anderson. However, rather than pay through the nose for a partner for Ibrahim Sangare of Xaver Schager, they opted to hand James McAtee a more prominent role as the former Crystal Palace boss sought to implement his favoured 3-4-2-1 system.

The 24-year-old departed Manchester City for Forest last summer, penning a five-year deal as the Nottingham powerhouse parted with £30m to sign the midfielder. To say McAtee struggled for regular first-team football would be a huge understatement. The former City trainee started just two league matches, making a further 12 Premier League outings off the bench.

It’s hardly a shock that playing time was hard to come by. Anderson was thriving in the middle of the park. Captain Morgan Gibbs-White was always going to be the first name on the teamsheet. The Europa League offered McAtee a chance for minutes, as he started nine European matches, but a lack of frequent action didn’t aid the Englishman.

Now, though, he’s gone on to become a mainstay in this Forest side under Glasner. 684 Premier League minutes is the fourth-most for Glasner’s side, with Matz Sels, Murillo (both 720) and Neco Williams (714) those to rank above McAtee. He’s taken to a role change with ease, filling the Anderson void in a way that few thought possible.

Using Gradient Sports, we can truly check to see if McAtee has replaced Anderson in the middle of the park. Crucial is finding the right player to operate as an effective distributor in the midfield of Glasner’s ideal setup. He wants one of the two in the middle of the park to act as a ball winner and another to set the tempo in a key area.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

In his final season at Selhurst Park, Glasner had either Will Hughes or Daichi Kamada feature alongside Adam Wharton. He wanted Wharton’s partner to shine off the ball in order to allow the England star to maximise his vision. Schlager is carrying out this role to an impressive standard, with the summer signing averaging 4.1 tackles per 90.

In terms of passing grade, Anderson ranked in the 97th percentile, with 81. By comparison, McAtee is in the 87th percentile for the same metric, with 73.9 to his name. Anderson, though, was very much the heartbeat of this side and had moulded his game accordingly to suit the needs of whichever manager was at the City Ground helm.

For context, McAtee has been forced to adapt his game to suit Glasner’s demands. Last season in particular, a bulk of his starts came in a more attacking role, either through the middle or out wide. He’s not yet matching Anderson, but has made a fine start.

McAtee has taken to a role change with ease, filling the Anderson void in a way that few thought possible

What’s key is that McAtee matches the one-touch passing gameplan that Wharton is famed for. The England star is renowned for his ability to take minimal touches before shifting possession on. McAtee is living up to that standard, and then some, with his one-touch passing grade sat at 73.3, which in itself is in the 87th percentile in England’s top tier this term. Last season, Anderson managed a grade of 73.4, a return that was in the 86th percentile in the 2025/26 Premier League season.

Anderson (643) managed more one-touch passing events than McAtee is managing in 2026/27 (122) but again - the latter has been forced to adjust his game.

Another key factor in McAtee replacing Anderson is the off-ball work. While there is a designated ball winner in the side, both midfielders need to pull their weight in a key area. This is where McAtee lets himself down a touch, with a tackling grade of 56.3 ranking in the 16th percentile. Anderson ranks far higher, with 72.7 in the 79th percentile for tackling grade.

In a 50/50 duel grade, McAtee is the one who fares better when it comes to winning possession against an opponent, 79 to Anderson’s 67.8, meaning you’d back the former to come out on top in a simultaneous opportunity to gain control of a loose or contested ball. There’s room for improvement, but McAtee is certainly on the right track.

So whether it’s clear that McAtee has fully replaced Anderson in the midfield, he has to an extent. What’s noticeable, though, is that McAtee has altered his game to play that Wharton quarter-back role. 102 forward passes is fourth for Forest. Remove the goalkeeper and centre-backs, and he jumps to top. He’s second to Gibbs-White for chances created (16). When Forest win the ball, they get it upfield quickly. Only Manchester United (15) have had more shots following a counter-attack than Glasner’s side (14) in the 2026/27 Premier League season.

The players have quickly bought into Glasner’s gameplan, even if the results haven’t quite gone their way. A xG underperformance of 10.28 is far and away the biggest in the Premier League. 16.28 xG generated is the third most in the division. Once it clicks, then Glasner’s side will move away from danger. And McAtee, as both a hybrid Anderson replacement combined with a new-age Wharton, is at the heart of everything Forest are now doing. 31 per cent of their attacks come through the middle of the pitch, the joint highest such proportion in the league, in part due to McAtee.

It’s been quite the change in fortunes for a midfielder who wasn’t getting a look in last season.

Having departed esteemed football stats website WhoScored.com in 2025, he has been touring the freelance circuit, and has been featured on The Guardian, The Independent, Sky Sports, The Premier League, TNT Sports, ESPN, Squawka and talkSPORT, among others.

Food contains thousands of overlooked chemicals. This physicist is mapping them

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

By Tina Hesman Saey, Mandana Tadayon and Anna Mattson

Computational physicist Giulia Menichetti is looking for “dark matter” in our food. In addition to known macronutrients, vitamins and minerals, the things we eat contain a whole world of natural chemicals we have no idea about. And those chemicals may affect our health.

“They have the ability to interact, to bind to human proteins, to modulate our microbiome,” says Menichetti of Harvard Medical School. “We want to leverage this to talk more and more about the role of food and nutrition in human health.”

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Menichetti leads Foodome, a project to map our food’s full chemical complexity. Her team uses AI to mine scientific papers for every chemical compound that has ever been linked to a food. She singles out ones that haven’t been tracked by epidemiological studies — the “nutritional dark matter.” So far, her team has cataloged about 140,000 molecules.

When one hears about chemicals in food, they might initially think of artificial additives. Her team addresses those, but she’s also interested in naturally occurring chemicals. Take garlic, for example. Foodome has cataloged almost 7,000 different molecules in raw garlic alone. Some of these compounds have been investigated for health effects, but some are entirely new.

Foodome is still mining the scientific literature for these molecules and building out databases. Eventually, Menichetti would like AI to be able to predict how all these food molecules interact together and in the human body.

“There is a legacy of chemical complexity that comes from organisms that are all around us,” she says. “Food is part of that. It is actually our way to interact with our environment. We need to study it better.”

SEC Updates Crypto-Asset FAQs as Firms Seek Clarity on Federal Securities Rules

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The SEC Division of Corporation Finance added questions 1.4, 1.5 and 1.6 to its crypto-assets FAQ on November 2, 2026. SEC staff positions may be informative without having the legal force of a statute, judicial judgment or final Commission rule.

This distinction is worth making because crypto regulation often arrives in several formats: speeches, staff statements, enforcement actions, proposals and final rules. They have different implications even when the subject is the same. A dated FAQ update merits attention without being promoted into a sweeping legal turning point.

The SEC Division of Corporation Finance added questions 1.4, 1.5 and 1.6 to its crypto-assets FAQ on November 2, 2026. The new staff answers address governance through foundations or organisations, what counts as control over participation and the difference between a functional network and a decentralised one. The page expressly says its answers represent staff views, have no legal force and have not been approved or disapproved by the Commission. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

A functioning crypto system can have a native asset that users need for programmatic activity without necessarily being decentralised. Under the staff explanation, decentralisation is a distinct question about whether any party or group holds operational, economic or voting control. A foundation or association involved in broad-based governance is not automatically a controlling central party; the actual powers and decision rules matter. This is a narrower, more useful clarification than saying every governance token has received a new legal classification.

The additions give lawyers and builders more concrete language for evaluating governance arrangements. A protocol may distribute votes widely while retaining privileged access, unilateral upgrade powers or concentrated economic influence. The staff view asks analysts to study those powers rather than rely on the mere existence of a formal foundation. That could influence how firms document their networks, although it does not eliminate case-by-case analysis or statutory legal requirements.

Crypto policy updates arrive as proposals, staff explanations, enforcement cases and final rules. Those documents carry different legal weight even when they use similar language. A careful article tells readers who issued the text, when it was published and whether it changes obligations now. That distinction is especially important for financial firms making compliance decisions.

This development also connects to two subjects our newsroom has been following: DTCC’s tokenization service and settlement controls and financial infrastructure and technology policy coverage. Those background reports help separate established facts from a new, still-developing announcement.

SEC staff positions may be informative without having the legal force of a statute, judicial judgment or final Commission rule. Investors should also resist the leap from regulatory discussion to token-price predictions. A frequently asked questions page is not an approval notice for any specific exchange, security or stablecoin.

Regulation ultimately affects operational choices: where customer assets are held, who can move them, how decisions are recorded and which risks are disclosed. The market can debate policy direction, but firms still need to map any published guidance to existing duties and the specific activity they carry out. General labels rarely answer those questions.

Watch for direct Commission statements, formal rulemaking notices, court decisions and clarifications that identify the affected transactions. Teams reviewing compliance can use the FAQ alongside primary legal sources, while documenting areas where further advice is necessary. Precision about who issued a document is essential to accurate policy reporting.

CISAC Reports Record Global Creator Royalties, Warns of ‘Largest Test’ From AI

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Digital is now the largest generator of publishing royalties, accounting for 45.6% of the total.

Global music publishing collections grew to nearly 14.6 billion euros ($15.89 billion) in 2025, up 11.9% from the prior year’s total of 12.6 billion euros ($13.62 billion), according to data published in CISAC’s latest Global Collections Report.

CISAC director general Gadi Oron noted that the growth demonstrates “both the resilience of collective management and the enduring demand for creative works around the world.” Related Kenny MacPherson, Veteran Music Publisher and Big Deal Music Co-Founder, Dies at 71 Jessica Lynch

However, more than half of the increase, or 6.6 percentage points was due to the first time inclusion of revenue from the Mechanical Licensing Collective (MLC), the U.S. collection agency, while only only about 5.3 percentage points came from organic growth. On the other hand, the 5.3% organic growth from publishing outpaced the 4.8% increase for 2025, as counted by IFPI, that the record label side of the music business experienced.

While the industry is still growing, a counterbalance worry is how the double-edged sword of artificial intelligence will impact the economic model.

“Our confederation now faces its largest test with artificial intelligence, new business models and changing patterns of consumption,” Oron writes in the forward to the CISAC annual report. “This technological change also means that the future can bring real opportunities for creators…The issue is no longer whether AI will transform our industries, but whether the systems that protect creators’ rights are evolving quickly enough to keep pace.”

Fortunately, CISAC and its member societies are at the center of the debate around new laws, and is “contributing to numerous consultations, policy debates and legislative processes around the world, so as to ensure that creators’ rights remain at the heart of AI policy making,” Oron wrote.

Adds CISAC president and ABBA co-founder Björn Ulvaeus, “Human creativity is not something to be replaced or bypassed. It is something to be valued, protected and celebrated. And I hope that if we get it right, artificial intelligence will not diminish human creativity but will help inspire its next chapter.”

Getting back to the numbers, digital is now the largest generator of publishing royalties comprising 45.6% of music publishing royalties. In 2025, digital royalties totaled 6.41 billion euros ($7.25 billion) or a 28% increase over the 5.01 billion euros ($5.42 billion) collected in 2024. But it is here where the additional of the MLC is having its biggest impact.

Otherwise, background music and concerts was second largest at 25.8% of music publishing revenue with a total of 3.624 billion euros ($4.1 billion), or a 7.2% increase over the prior year when that category total 3.38 billion euros ($3.66 billion).

All other categories of publishing royalties produced declines in 2025 when compared to 2024. For instance, television and radio brought in another 23.6% of total publishing revenue in 2025 with 3.315 billion euros ($3.75 billion), but that was down 3.1% from the prior year total of 3.42 billion euros ($3.67 billion.) Similarly, CDs and video produced 326 million euros ($358.6 million), or a 8.9% drop on the 358 million euros ($387.3 million) garnered in 2024; while private copying assessments brought in 252 million euros ($285 million), or a 6.7% decrease from the prior year’s total of 270 million euros ($292.1 million); sync also declined slightly to 58 million euros ($65.6 million) from 62 million euros (467 million); and all other licensing sources generated 70 million euros ($79.15 million) or a 24.1% decline from 92.25 million euros ($99.8 million).

Looking at revenue by the continents, Western Europe brought in the most revenue, accounting for 45.2% of revenue with 6.353 billion euros ($7.2 billion emanating from these territories); with North America coming in second, comprising 31.6% of total revenue with 4.442 billion euros ($5.02 million). Asia-Pacific with 1.87 billion euros ($2.12 billion) was third largest at 13.3% of royalties; followed by Latin America at 5.6%, or 785 million euros ($887.6 million); Central and Eastern Europe with 529 million euros ($598 million), or 3.8%; and Africa with 77 million euros ($87.1 million), which was one half a percent.

Within the continents, looking at the five largest countries for music publishing royalties, the United States accounted for 4.073 billion ($4.61 billion); France 1.59 billion euros ($1.8 billion); the U.K with 1.283 billion euros ($1.45 billion); Germany at 1.05 billion euros ($1.185 billion) and Japan at 907 million euros ($1.025 billion).

In addition to music, the collection management organizations also collect royalties on literature, drama, visual arts and audiovisual and when those revenues are added in CISAC counted total revenue at 15.5 billion euros ($17.53 billion), or a 11.3% increase over the prior year’s 13.92 billion euros ($15.06 billion).

CISAC represents 225 collective management organizations located in 110 countries and territories. Looking ahead, Oron noted “As CISAC approaches its second century, our purpose remains to strengthen the international framework that recognizes creators’ rights, supports effective collective management and enables creativity to continue flourishing in an increasingly interconnected world.”

Read more at cisac.org and download the report here.

The Specific Pain of Outgrowing an Adult Friendship

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For my 30th birthday, I hosted a game show about me. It was a ridiculous affair held in West London, where people that I’ve known and loved through the years participated in a series of rounds including: a rap round where guests had to write and perform a verse over Skepta and JME’s “That’s Not Me”; an arts and crafts round where the best drawing of me won; a music round featuring songs from the year I was born; and, of course, a “defend your embarrassing boyfriend” round. The lead-up to 30 was stressful in a way I can’t help but cringe at now; the age carries a symbolic weight that I had attached the pressure of completeness to, as if I’d be cemented at 30 and never change. Despite these irrational fears, my 30th was probably the best celebration I’d ever thrown. After years of dragging friends through intense physical activities—zip-lining, gymnastics, pole, and even aerial skills—a game show felt like a lighter option, something everyone could enjoy.

Still, between the singing, dancing, and early morning revelry, a low hum of sadness accompanied me. There was something missing. It wasn’t that I didn’t appreciate the friends and family who’d come to take part in yet another of my mad ideas; I just felt haunted by the absence of the friends whom I’d assumed would see the day with me. Friends I used to speak to daily—sharing everything from the mundane details of what I had for breakfast to the intricacies of my menstrual cycle—weren’t there for a milestone celebration. It would be easy to say that these friendships had tumultuous endings with clean breaks, where the lines of right and wrong were clearly drawn, but they weren’t, which made it even more painful.

The specific experience of grieving a friendship break-up that cannot be located in a single event is a form of “frozen grief,” a term coined by psychologist Pauline Boss. You are frozen when you cannot process such a loss because its cause is unclear.

Friendship fallouts are an endless source of fuel for the content factory. Women and girls applying makeup or skincare while revealing the story of a best friend who betrayed them can temporarily break the doomscroll. The storyteller is always the victim, the former “best friend” always the villain, and it falls into an easily digestible narrative even when the poster asks, “Am I the problem here?” I often wonder how the breakdown of years-long, nuanced, and complex friendships can be reduced to short clips that portray ex-friends as cartoonishly evil, stripped of their humanity. That said, I do secretly envy posters like this; they have a story, a timeline they can reliably use to trace the end of a friendship.

What I lacked in clarity consumed me with sadness. Kat Vellos, author of We Should Get Together and founder of community program Platonic Action Lab, describes friendship as blooming from four seeds of connection: proximity, frequency, compatibility, and commitment. When the end of a friendship cannot be traced to a single moment of betrayal, she says, it can often be attributed to the absence of one of these seeds.

In my case, certain friendships have fizzled because groups have splintered and formed friendships with other groups, creating new ecosystems I wasn’t part of. It can be hard to trace where things really ended, but sometimes I can remember the last proper conversation we ever had. My friend Fi and I spoke every day, sometimes multiple times—but during busy periods, this happened much less often. A week prior, we’d scheduled a hangout. Nothing out of the ordinary, just a catch-up. It was a busy week; we hadn’t FaceTimed as normal. I texted her on the day to figure out what we’d be doing. She replied instantly, “Sorry, girl, I didn’t hear from you. I’m out with my other friend tonight.” And that was that. We never usually confirmed ahead of time; I’d assumed our friendship was fluid and existed without that level of admin, especially for arranged plans. Still, we never really spoke again. I messaged her on her birthday, but she didn’t message me on mine, and when we saw each other in person, once, it didn’t feel like “no time had passed.” It felt like I’d lived 40 lives since our last conversation, and things were never the same.

The older I get, the fuzzier the image of me with a partner and kids is in my mind’s eye. I’m unsure if my future will involve being focused on a traditional family unit and because of that, my friendships are of utmost importance to me. Friends are not consolation prizes for romantic relationships; they are equally significant, if not even more so. The loss of a friendship feels like the loss of an imagined future we shared. It is the loss of a support system, and it feels even worse when there is no big lesson to be learned from it, no great “wrong” to work through—just an unsatisfying understanding that things change, and sometimes it is beyond our control to stop them.

Though I felt sad at points on my birthday, I was also grateful for all the new relationships that spawned in the wake of old ones; new rituals created and memories shared. There was also something to learn in the grief, as Elizabeth Day writes in Friendaholic: “Perhaps in the letting go, we had understood something integral about ourselves. Perhaps that was all right. Perhaps that was a kind of love.”
